Oh, um... I wouldn't say I'm *very* concerned, exactly. I just... I really want to make sure I can do my job well, you know?

Well, um... I guess I do try to be a little careful about certain things. I mean, I wouldn't say I avoid them completely because I'm worried about my weight exactly, but... 

I just find that if I eat something really heavy, like, say, a couple of slices of pizza or a big, thick sandwich for lunch, I get this... this kind of panicky feeling. Like I'm going to wake up the next day feeling bigger and slower. And with my job, I really need to be light on my feet, you know? I have to be able to move around ladders and squeeze into tight spaces in rooms. 

So, I just tend to stick to lighter things that give me energy without making me feel weighed down. It just seems like the smart thing to do for my work. Does that make sense? I hope that doesn't sound like I'm being too restrictive or anything... I just want to make sure I'm always ready to do a good job.

Well, um... like I was just mentioning with the pizza or the sandwiches, it really is mostly about that... that panicky feeling I get.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... I feel like we might be going in circles a little bit, but I really don't mind explaining it again if it helps you understand. I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need. 

Like I was saying, it's not so much about the word "fat." It's more about that... that panic I feel. If I have something heavy, like a big sandwich or a couple slices of pizza, I just get this overwhelming fear that I'm going to wake up the next morning and be bigger, and... and just too slow. And if I'm bigger, I won't be able to scramble up and down my ladders or squeeze behind furniture when I'm papering a room. 

So, I guess... yes, I am afraid of getting bigger, but it's really just because I need to be physically capable for my work. I'm not trying to be difficult or avoid your question, I promise. Does that help clarify things a bit better? I just want to make sure I'm doing this right.

I don't really have the time or energy to go to a gym and work out on top of all that. \n\nI just try to be sensible, I guess. I hope that's the right answer? I really wouldn't want you to think I was doing anything extreme or... or unhealthy. I just try to keep a good balance.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you've looked in the mirror and felt like you needed to be smaller, even if you knew logically that you weren't overweight?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think I've ever really had a moment quite like that. \n\nI mean, I look in the mirror to make sure I don't have paint in my hair before I leave the house, or to check that my uniform is clean, but... I try not to scrutinize myself too much. I've always thought it's better to focus on what my body can do—like lifting the heavy rollers and reaching the high corners—rather than getting caught up in trying to be smaller. \n\nI just want to be strong enough to do my job well. That's the most important thing, right? I really try to keep a healthy mindset about it all. I hope that's what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever judged yourself primarily based on your body shape or weight, even if others might see you as being at a normal or low weight?\n\nPatient: Um... no, I really try my hardest not to do that. I'd hate to be the kind of person who only values themselves based on, you know, a number on a scale or how their clothes fit. \n\nLike I mentioned earlier, I really prefer to judge myself on my character and my work ethic. I just want to make sure I'm being helpful and giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Are there certain types of foods you avoid eating altogether because you're worried they might cause you to gain weight?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I'm not entirely sure if I'm explaining it right, or if that's what you meant...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit, used laxatives, or exercised excessively to try to control your weight or the shape of your body?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I would never do anything like that. I mean, making myself sick or taking pills... that sounds really dangerous, and I definitely wouldn't want to do anything to mess up my health. I need to be in good shape for work, you know? \n\nAs for exercising... well, my job is pretty physical. Like I said, I'm up and down ladders, carrying paint buckets, moving furniture... so I get a lot of a workout just doing that. I don't really have the time or energy to go to a gym and work out on top of all that. \n\nI just try to be sensible, I guess. I hope that's the right answer? I really wouldn't want you to think I was doing anything extreme or... or unhealthy. I just try to keep a good balance.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you've looked in the mirror and felt like you needed to be smaller, even if you knew logically that you weren't overweight?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think I've ever really had a moment quite like that. \n\nI mean, I look in the mirror to make sure I don't have paint in my hair before I leave the house, or to check that my uniform is clean, but... I try not to scrutinize myself too much. I've always thought it's better to focus on what my body can do—like lifting the heavy rollers and reaching the high corners—rather than getting caught up in trying to be smaller. \n\nI just want to be strong enough to do my job well. That's the most important thing, right? I really try to keep a healthy mindset about it all. I hope that's what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever judged yourself primarily based on your body shape or weight, even if others might see you as being at a normal or low weight?\n\nPatient: Um... no, I really try my hardest not to do that. I definitely wouldn't say I judge my whole worth on my weight or my shape. I really try to keep a positive attitude about it all. Is... is that the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm being helpful and giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Are there certain types of foods you avoid eating altogether because you're worried they might cause you to gain weight?\n\nPatient: Well, um... Like I'm going to wake up the next day feeling bigger and slower. And with my job, I really need to be light on my feet, you know? I just get so scared that I'll wake up bigger and slower, and then I won't be able to move around my ladders and the rooms the way I need to for my work. \n\nIs... is that what you were looking for? I really hope I answered it right this time. I'm so sorry for making you repeat yourself again, I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Outside of concerns related to your job performance, do you ever worry about gaining weight or becoming bigger in everyday situations?\n\nPatient: Um... outside of work? Well... I try not to think about it too much, honestly. I don't want to be one of those people who is always obsessing over every little thing, you know? That just seems so... I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about becoming bigger, is it specifically because you believe it would interfere with your ability to do your job, or do you also worry about being bigger in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, wow, I really feel like I'm frustrating you, and I am so, so sorry. I must sound like a broken record, and I know you're just trying to do your job. I promise I'm not trying to be evasive or anything. \n\nUm... I guess it really does just come back to the work thing. Like I was saying, if I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or pizza, I just get this... this really intense, panicky feeling. I just get so scared that I'll wake up the next day bigger and slower, and then I won't be able to move around my ladders and the rooms the way I need to. \n\nI know it sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but that's really the main thing I worry about. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh... I see. I'm so sorry, I think I finally understand what you're asking now. Thank you for being so patient with me, I know I haven't been doing the best job of explaining myself. 

Um, to be completely honest... not really? I mean, I really try not to care too much about what other people think of my shape when I'm just out and about. I feel like it would be a bit... I mostly just value being a hard worker and being reliable. \n\nI hope that answers your question? I'm not entirely sure if I'm explaining it right, or if that's what you meant...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit, used laxatives, or exercised excessively to try to control your weight or the shape of your body?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I would never do anything like that. I mean, making myself sick or taking pills... that sounds really dangerous, and I definitely wouldn't want to do anything to mess up my health. I need to be in good shape for work, you know? \n\nAs for exercising... well, my job is pretty physical. Like I said, I'm up and down ladders, carrying paint buckets, moving furniture... so I get a lot of a workout just doing that. I don't really have the time or energy to go to a gym and work out on top of all that. \n\nI just try to be sensible, I guess. I hope that's the right answer? I really wouldn't want you to think I was doing anything extreme or... or unhealthy. I just try to keep a good balance.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you've looked in the mirror and felt like you needed to be smaller, even if you knew logically that you weren't overweight?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think I've ever really had a moment quite like that. \n\nI mean, I look in the mirror to make sure I don't have paint in my hair before I leave the house, or to check that my uniform is clean, but... I try not to scrutinize myself too much. I've always thought it's better to focus on what my body can do—like lifting the heavy rollers and reaching the high corners—rather than getting caught up in trying to be smaller. \n\nI just want to be strong enough to do my job well. That's the most important thing, right? I really try to keep a healthy mindset about it all. I hope that's what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever judged yourself primarily based on your body shape or weight, even if others might see you as being at a normal or low weight?\n\nPatient: Um... no, I really try my hardest not to do that. I'd hate to be the kind of person who only values themselves based on, you know, a number on a scale or how their clothes fit. \n\nLike I mentioned earlier, I really prefer to judge myself on my character and my work ethic. Am I doing a good job? Am I showing up on time for my clients? That kind of thing. \n\nI mean, I think everyone has days where they feel a little less confident, but... I definitely wouldn't say I judge my whole worth on my weight or my shape. I really try to keep a positive attitude about it all. Is... is that the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm being helpful and giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Are there certain types of foods you avoid eating altogether because you're worried they might cause you to gain weight?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I do try to be a little careful about certain things. I mean, I wouldn't say I avoid them completely because I'm worried about my weight exactly, but... \n\nI just find that if I eat something really heavy, like, say, a couple of slices of pizza or a big, thick sandwich for lunch, I get this... this kind of panicky feeling. Like I'm going to wake up the next day feeling bigger and slower. And with my job, I really need to be light on my feet, you know? I have to be able to move around ladders and squeeze into tight spaces in rooms. \n\nSo, I just tend to stick to lighter things that give me energy without making me feel weighed down. It just seems like the smart thing to do for my work. Does that make sense? I hope that doesn't sound like I'm being too restrictive or anything... I just want to make sure I'm always ready to do a good job.\n\nDoctor: When you choose not to eat certain heavier foods, is it mainly because you're afraid of gaining weight or becoming fat, even if you know logically that you might not need to lose weight?\n\nPatient: Well, um... like I was just mentioning with the pizza or the sandwiches, it really is mostly about that... that panicky feeling I get. \n\nI wouldn't want to use the word \"fat,\" exactly, because that sounds so... I don't know, superficial? And I'd hate for you to think I'm just being vain about my appearance. I really try not to be like that. But... I guess, if I'm being completely honest, yes. I do get really anxious that if I eat those heavier things, I'm going to wake up bigger. And if I'm bigger and slower, I just won't be able to move around my ladders or fit into the tight spaces when I'm painting or hanging wallpaper. \n\nSo I guess... maybe it is a fear of getting bigger? But it really stems from just wanting to be capable and good at my job. I hope that makes sense? I'm really trying to answer as best as I can, I just want to make sure I'm explaining it right so I don't give you the wrong impression.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid eating heavier foods, would you say it's mainly due to a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even if you know you might not actually need to lose weight?\n\nPatient: Um... I feel like we might be going in circles a little bit, but I really don't mind explaining it again if it helps you understand. I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need. \n\nLike I was saying, it's not so much about the word \"fat.\" It's more about that... that panic I feel. If I have something heavy, like a big sandwich or a couple slices of pizza, I just get this overwhelming fear that I'm going to wake up the next morning and be bigger, and... and just too slow. And if I'm bigger, I won't be able to scramble up and down my ladders or squeeze behind furniture when I'm papering a room. \n\nSo, I guess... yes, I am afraid of getting bigger, but it's really just because I need to be physically capable for my work. I'm not trying to be difficult or avoid your question, I promise. Does that help clarify things a bit better? I just want to make sure I'm doing this right.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your decision to avoid heavier foods is mainly driven by a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even if you know that you might not actually need to lose weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh... I'm so sorry. I feel like I must be frustrating you since you have to keep asking me the exact same question. I must be doing a really bad job of explaining myself, and I promise I'm not trying to be difficult or avoid what you're asking. \n\nUm... yes. I guess the simplest answer is yes. Even though logically I know I probably don't need to lose anything, if I eat something heavy like pizza or a big sandwich, I just get this... this really awful, panicky feeling. I just get so scared that I'll wake up bigger and slower, and then I won't be able to move around my ladders and the rooms the way I need to for my work. \n\nIs... is that what you were looking for? I really hope I answered it right this time. I'm so sorry for making you repeat yourself again, I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Outside of concerns related to your job performance, do you ever worry about gaining weight or becoming bigger in everyday situations?\n\nPatient: Um... outside of work? Well... I try not to think about it too much, honestly. I don't want to be one of those people who is always obsessing over every little thing, you know? That just seems so... I don't know, self-centered? And I really try to be mindful of that.\n\nBut, um... I guess... sometimes? Like, if I'm trying on clothes or getting ready to go out, I might get a little nervous if something feels tighter than it used to. But I think that's pretty normal, isn't it? Most women probably feel that way sometimes. I just... I really try to focus on being healthy and capable, rather than just how I look. \n\nI hope that's an okay answer? I'm not really sure if I'm explaining it right, or if that's what you meant by everyday situations. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about becoming bigger, is it specifically because you believe it would interfere with your ability to do your job, or do you also worry about being bigger in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, wow, I really feel like I'm frustrating you, and I am so, so sorry. I must sound like a broken record, and I know you're just trying to do your job. I promise I'm not trying to be evasive or anything. \n\nUm... I guess it really does just come back to the work thing. Like I was saying, if I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or pizza, I just get this... this really intense, panicky feeling. I just get so scared that I'll wake up the next day bigger and slower, and then I won't be able to move around my ladders and the rooms the way I need to. \n\nI know it sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but that's really the main thing I worry about. I just want to be good at what I do, you know? I hope that makes sense. Is that... is that the kind of answer you were looking for? I really want to be helpful here.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your concerns about job performance, do you ever feel anxious about your body shape or weight affecting how you look or how others see you in non-work-related situations?\n\nPatient: Oh... I see. I'm so sorry, I think I finally understand what you're asking now. Thank you for being so patient with me, I know I haven't been doing the best job of explaining myself. \n\nUm, to be completely honest... not really? I mean, I really try not to care too much about what other people think of my shape when I'm just out and about. I feel like it would be a bit... I don't know, superficial? To worry about how I look at the grocery store or if I'm seeing friends. I'd much rather people just think I'm a hard worker and a nice person, you know? \n\nSo, no, I don't really get anxious about my weight just for the sake of looking good for other people. It truly just comes back to being light enough to do my painting and paperhanging properly. \n\nDoes... does that answer the question better? I really hope I'm giving you what you need this time.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about gaining weight limited entirely to how it might affect your ability to do your job, without worrying about it in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. Um... I really hope I'm not sounding difficult or stubborn by repeating myself, but yes, it is entirely about my job. I just don't really focus on it in other areas of my life. As long as I can get up on my ladders and do my work without feeling heavy or slow, I'm okay. \n\nI'm so sorry if it took me a few tries to give you a straight answer on that. I just want to make sure I'm doing this right and being as helpful as possible. Does that... does that give you what you need for your notes?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear of gaining weight is exclusively related to how it might impact your ability to do your job, and not connected to how you view your body in general or how you think others might see you outside of work?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh... yes. Like I'm going to wake up the next day feeling bigger and slower. And with my job, I really need to be light on my feet, you know? I have to be able to move around ladders and squeeze into tight spaces in rooms. \n\nSo, I just tend to stick to lighter things that give me energy without making me feel weighed down. I just try to keep a good balance.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you've looked in the mirror and felt like you needed to be smaller, even if you knew logically that you weren't overweight?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think I've ever really had a moment quite like that. \n\nI mean, I look in the mirror to make sure I don't have paint in my hair before I leave the house, or to check that my uniform is clean, but... I try not to scrutinize myself too much. I've always thought it's better to focus on what my body can do—like lifting the heavy rollers and reaching the high corners—rather than getting caught up in trying to be smaller. \n\nI just want to be strong enough to do my job well. That's the most important thing, right? I really try to keep a healthy mindset about it all. I hope that's what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever judged yourself primarily based on your body shape or weight, even if others might see you as being at a normal or low weight?\n\nPatient: Um... no, I really try my hardest not to do that. I'd hate to be the kind of person who only values themselves based on, you know, a number on a scale or how their clothes fit. \n\nLike I mentioned earlier, I really prefer to judge myself on my character and my work ethic. Am I doing a good job? Am I showing up on time for my clients? That kind of thing. \n\nI mean, I think everyone has days where they feel a little less confident, but... I definitely wouldn't say I judge my whole worth on my weight or my shape. I really try to keep a positive attitude about it all. Is... is that the kind of answer you were looking for? Like I'm going to wake up the next day feeling bigger and slower. And with my job, I really need to be light on my feet, you know? I have to be able to move around ladders and squeeze into tight spaces in rooms. \n\nSo, I just tend to stick to lighter things that give me energy without making me feel weighed down. It just seems like the smart thing to do for my work. Does that make sense? I hope that doesn't sound like I'm being too restrictive or anything... I just want to make sure I'm always ready to do a good job.\n\nDoctor: When you choose not to eat certain heavier foods, is it mainly because you're afraid of gaining weight or becoming fat, even if you know logically that you might not need to lose weight?\n\nPatient: Well, um... like I was just mentioning with the pizza or the sandwiches, it really is mostly about that... that panicky feeling I get. \n\nI wouldn't want to use the word \"fat,\" exactly, because that sounds so... I'm really trying to answer as best as I can, I just want to make sure I'm explaining it right so I don't give you the wrong impression.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid eating heavier foods, would you say it's mainly due to a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even if you know you might not actually need to lose weight?\n\nPatient: Um... I'd hate to be the kind of person who only values themselves based on, you know, a number on a scale or how their clothes fit. \n\nLike I mentioned earlier, I really prefer to judge myself on my character and my work ethic. Am I doing a good job? Am I showing up on time for my clients? That kind of thing. \n\nI mean, I think everyone has days where they feel a little less confident, but... I definitely wouldn't say I judge my whole worth on my weight or my shape. I really try to keep a positive attitude about it all. Is... is that the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm being helpful and giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Are there certain types of foods you avoid eating altogether because you're worried they might cause you to gain weight?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I do try to be a little careful about certain things. I mean, I wouldn't say I avoid them completely because I'm worried about my weight exactly, but... \n\nI just find that if I eat something really heavy, like, say, a couple of slices of pizza or a big, thick sandwich for lunch, I get this... this kind of panicky feeling. Like I'm going to wake up the next day feeling bigger and slower. And with my job, I really need to be light on my feet, you know? I have to be able to move around ladders and squeeze into tight spaces in rooms. \n\nSo, I just tend to stick to lighter things that give me energy without making me feel weighed down. It just seems like the smart thing to do for my work. Does that make sense? I hope that doesn't sound like I'm being too restrictive or anything... I just want to make sure I'm always ready to do a good job.\n\nDoctor: When you choose not to eat certain heavier foods, is it mainly because you're afraid of gaining weight or becoming fat, even if you know logically that you might not need to lose weight?\n\nPatient: Well, um... like I was just mentioning with the pizza or the sandwiches, it really is mostly about that... that panicky feeling I get. \n\nI wouldn't want to use the word \"fat,\" exactly, because that sounds so... I don't know, superficial? And I'd hate for you to think I'm just being vain about my appearance. I really try not to be like that. But... I guess, if I'm being completely honest, yes. I do get really anxious that if I eat those heavier things, I'm going to wake up bigger. And if I'm bigger and slower, I just won't be able to move around my ladders or fit into the tight spaces when I'm painting or hanging wallpaper. \n\nSo I guess... maybe it is a fear of getting bigger? But it really stems from just wanting to be capable and good at my job. I hope that makes sense? I'm really trying to answer as best as I can, I just want to make sure I'm explaining it right so I don't give you the wrong impression.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid eating heavier foods, would you say it's mainly due to a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even if you know you might not actually need to lose weight?\n\nPatient: Um... I feel like we might be going in circles a little bit, but I really don't mind explaining it again if it helps you understand. And if I'm bigger, I won't be able to scramble up and down my ladders or squeeze behind furniture when I'm papering a room. \n\nSo, I guess... yes, I am afraid of getting bigger, but it's really just because I need to be physically capable for my work. I'm not trying to be difficult or avoid your question, I promise. Does that help clarify things a bit better? I just want to make sure I'm doing this right.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your decision to avoid heavier foods is mainly driven by a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even if you know that you might not actually need to lose weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh... I don't know, self-centered? And I really try to be mindful of that.\n\nBut, um... I guess... sometimes? Like, if I'm trying on clothes or getting ready to go out, I might get a little nervous if something feels tighter than it used to. But I think that's pretty normal, isn't it? I'm not really sure if I'm explaining it right, or if that's what you meant by everyday situations.
